What does `apply-primitive` do with the Python function?\n\nIf `apply-primitive` does `(proc (car args) (car (cdr args)))`, then:\n- `proc` is the Python function\n- `(car args)` is 1, `(car (cdr args))` is 2\n- This creates `(Python-plus 1 2)`\n- eval: Python function + values -> my-apply -> apply-primitive -> (proc args) -> INFINITE LOOP\n\nSo the issue persists. Every call to a Python function goes through my-apply, which needs to handle it, but can't without infinite recursion.\n\nThe ONLY way to break this: have apply-primitive NOT go through my-apply. Let me think about the data structures:\n\n**Token types:**\n- Integer\n- Symbol (string)\n- String (with quotes)\n- Boolean (#t, #f)\n- Parentheses: '(' and ')'\n- Quote: '\\''\n\n**Pairs/Lists:** These are built-in (cons, car, cdr, null?, pair?)\n\n**Environment:** Association list mapping symbols to values, with parent pointer for closures.\n\n**Evaluation:**\n- Self-evaluating: numbers, booleans, strings\n- Symbols: look up in environment\n- Special forms: quote, if, cond, define, set!, lambda, let, begin/progn\n- Application: evaluate operator and operands, apply procedure\n\nLet me plan the implementation:\n\n1. **Primitives available from interp.py:** +, -, *, /, =, <, >, <=, >=, cons, car, cdr, null?, pair?, eq?, equal?, not, and, or, getchr, putchr, chr, read, symbol, symbol?, string?, number?, set-car!, set-cdr!, display, newline, fopen, fclose, fgetchr, fputchr, fdisplay, fread, else\n\n2. **Data structures in Scheme:**\n   - Environment: represented as a list `(parent-alist . bindings)` where parent-alist points to parent environment and bindings is an association list\n   \n   Actually, let me think about this more carefully. The environment needs to support:\n   - define (add to current bindings)\n   - set! (find and update in current or parent)\n   - lookup (find in current or parent)\n   \n   I can represent an environment as: `(parent . alist)` where `alist` is `((name . value) (name . value) ...)`\n\n3. **Core evaluator functions:**\n   - `(tokenize str)` -> list of tokens\n   - `(parse tokens)` -> expression\n   - `(eval-expr expr env)` -> value\n   - `(apply-proc proc args)` -> value\n   \n4. **Special form handling in eval:**\n   - quote: return expression as-is\n   - if: evaluate condition, then evaluate consequent or alternative\n   - cond: chain of if-else\n   - define: add to environment\n   - set!: update existing binding\n   - lambda: create procedure (closure)\n   - let: create new environment, evaluate body\n   - begin/progn: evaluate sequence\n\nLet me think about how to represent procedures. Here's the plan:\n\n```scheme\n;;; Metacircular evaluator for Scheme-like language\n\n;; ========== Environment operations ==========\n\n;; Create an environment\n(define (make-env parent)\n  (cons parent '()))\n\n;; Define a variable in the current environment\n(define (env-define env name value)\n  (set-cdr! env (cons (cons name value) (cdr env))))\n\n;; Look up a variable's value\n(define (env-lookup env name)\n  (let ((binding (assoc name (cdr env))))\n    (if binding\n        (cdr binding)\n        (if (null? (car obj) 'closure)))\n\n(define (closure-params closure) (car (cdr closure)))\n(define (closure-body closure) (car (cdr (cdr closure))))\n(define (closure-env closure) (car (cdr (cdr (cdr closure)))))\n```\n\nThen `apply-proc`:\n```scheme\n(define (apply-proc proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((closure? proc)\n     (let ((new-env (extend-env (closure-env proc)\n                                (closure-params proc)\n                                args)))\n       (eval-begin (closure-body proc) new-env)))\n    (else\n     (apply-primitive proc args))))\n\n(define (apply-primitive proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((null? args) (apply-primitive-0 proc))\n    ((null? (cdr args)) (apply-primitive-1 proc (car args)))\n    ((null? (cddr args)) (apply-primitive-2 proc (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ((null? (cdddr args)) (apply-primitive-3 proc (car args) (car (cdr args)) (car (cddr args))))\n    (else \n     ;; 4+ args, use the 4-arg primitive and recursively wrap\n     ...)))\n```\n\nFor more than 3 args, I can't easily apply. The metacircular evaluator needs to:\n\n1. Have its own global environment that includes all primitives\n2. Evaluate the program file in this environment\n3. My `eval.scm` code needs to build an evaluator that can be used for the program file.\n\nLet me design this step by step.\n\n### Design\n\n```\n1. Read filename from stdin\n2. Build the global environment with primitives\n3. Read and evaluate expressions from the file\n4. Output results\n```\n\n### Environment\n\nAn environment is a pair: `(bindings . parent)`.\n\n- `bindings` is an alist: `((name1 . value1) (name2 . value2) ...)`\n- `parent` is another environment or `'()` for the global\n\nHelper functions:\n```scheme\n(define (make-env parent) (cons '() parent))\n(define (env-bindings env) (car env))\n(define (env-parent env) (cdr env))\n(define (env-define env name value)\n  (set-car! env (cons (cons name value) (car env))))\n(define (env-set env name value)\n  (define (find-and-set bindings)\n    (cond ((null? bindings) #f)\n          ((eq? (car (car bindings)) name)\n           (set-cdr! (car bindings) value)\n           #t)\n          (else (find-and-set (cdr bindings)))))\n  (or (find-and-set (car env))\n      (if (not (null? (cdr env)))\n          (env-set (cdr env) name value)\n          (error \"Unbound variable\"))))\n(define (env-get env name)\n  (define (find bindings)\n    (cond ((null? bindings) #f)\n          ((eq? (car (car bindings)) name)\n           (cdr (car bindings)))\n          (else (find (cdr bindings)))))\n  (or (find (car env))\n      (if (not (null? (cdr env)))\n          (env-get (cdr env) name)\n          (error \"Unbound variable\"))))\n```\n\n### Procedure\n\nA user-defined procedure is represented as: `(procedure params body env)` where:\n- `params` is a list of parameter names\n- `body` is a list of expressions\n- `env` is the enclosing environment\n\n```scheme\n(define (make-procedure params body env)\n  (list 'procedure params body env))\n(define (procedure? obj)\n  (and (pair? obj) (eq? (car obj) 'procedure)))\n(define (procedure-params proc) (car (cdr proc)))\n(define (procedure-body proc) (car (cdr (cdr proc))))\n(define (procedure-env proc) (car (cdr (cdr (cdr proc)))))\n```\n\n### Evaluation\n\n```scheme\n(define (m-eval expr env)\n  (cond\n    ;; Self-evaluating\n    ((number? expr) expr)\n    ((string? expr) expr)\n    \n    ;; Boolean or null\n    ((eq? expr #t) #t)\n    ((eq? expr #f) #f)\n    ((null? expr) '())\n    \n    ;; Symbol (variable)\n    ((symbol? expr) (env-get env expr))\n    \n    ;; Quoted expression\n    ((tagged-list? expr 'quote) (car (cdr expr)))\n    \n    ;; Assignment\n    ((tagged-list? expr 'set!)\n     (env-set env (car (cdr expr)) (m-eval (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env)))\n    \n    ;; Definition\n    ((tagged-list? expr 'define)\n     (if (pair? (car (cdr expr)))\n         (define-function (car (cdr expr)) (cdr (cdr expr)) env)\n         (env-define env (car (cdr expr)) (m-eval (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env))))\n    \n    ;; If\n    ((tagged-list? expr 'if)\n     (if (m-eval (car (cdr expr)) env)\n         (m-eval (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env)\n         (if (not (null? (cdr (cdr (cdr expr)))))\n             (m-eval (car (cdr (cdr (cdr expr)))) env)\n             '())))\n    \n    ;; Lambda\n    ((tagged-list? expr 'lambda)\n     (make-procedure (car (cdr expr)) (cdr (cdr expr)) env))\n    \n    ;; Begin\n    ((tagged-list? expr 'begin)\n     (eval-sequence (cdr expr) env))\n    \n    ;; Cond\n    ((tagged-list? expr 'cond)\n     (m-eval (cond->if (cdr expr)) env))\n    \n    ;; Let\n    ((tagged-list? expr 'let)\n     (let ((bindings (car (cdr expr)))\n           (body (cdr (cdr expr))))\n       (m-eval (let->lambda bindings body) env)))\n    \n    ;; Application (function call)\n    (else\n     (m-apply (m-eval (car expr) env)\n              (eval-list (cdr expr) env)))))\n```\n\nWait, for the `let` transformation: `(let ((x 1) (y 2)) body)` becomes `((lambda (x y) body) 1 2)`. But when I'm inside the metacircular evaluator, I'm evaluating Scheme code. The `let->lambda` transformation creates a `lambda` expression that then gets evaluated. This is fine.\n\nBut wait - when I evaluate this with my `m-eval`, the `lambda` expression creates a procedure with the current `env`. The `let` should create a new environment with the bindings. This is correct for lexical scoping.\n\nActually, I think I can simplify by not transforming `let` and just handling it directly. Let me handle it directly in m-eval:\n\nFor `let`:\n```scheme\n(let ((x 1) (y 2)) body1 body2)\n```\nshould:\n1. Evaluate the value expressions (1 and 2) in the current environment\n2. (cdr body))\n                  (car body)\n                  (cons 'begin body)))\n        (extract-vals bindings)))\n```\n\n### Define function shorthand\n\n```scheme\n(define (define-function name-and-params body env)\n  (define name (car name-and-params))\n  (define params (cdr name-and-params))\n  (env-define env name (make-procedure params body env)))\n```\n\n### Application\n\n```scheme\n(define (m-apply proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((primitive-procedure? proc) (apply-primitive proc args))\n    ((compound-procedure? proc)\n     (eval-sequence\n      (procedure-body proc)\n      (extend-environment (procedure-params proc) args (procedure-env proc))))\n    (else (error \"Unknown procedure type\"))))\n```\n\nBut wait - primitive procedures are the ones defined by interp.py (like +, -, display, etc.). When the inner apply-primitive tries to call `(string? I need to ensure that inside `apply-primitive`, the symbol `string?` resolves to interp.py's primitive, not to my tagged primitive.\n\nOne way: don't put the tagged primitives in the same environment as the `apply-primitive` function. (car obj) 'procedure)))\n```\n\nA Python function is not a Pair, so `(pair? proc)` returns False. Good, `procedure?` returns False. Then the else branch:\n```scheme\n(proc (car args) (car (cdr args)))\n```\n\nThe outer m-eval evaluates this as a regular Scheme application. `proc` is a symbol, evaluated → Python function. `(car args)` → first arg. `(car (cdr args))` → second arg. Then interp.py calls `proc(val1, val2)` because `callable(proc)`.\n\nTHIS WORKS! No infinite loop. The Python function is a callable, and interp.py applies it directly.\n\nSo the solution is: store raw Python functions in my environment, not `(primitive name)` tags. In m-apply, check if the proc is a compound procedure (a list starting with 'procedure). If it's not, assume it's a callable and let interp.py handle it.\n\nBut wait - for the self-interpretation case, when the inner m-apply is called, it also needs to handle this. The inner m-apply is a compound procedure in my representation. When the outer m-eval applies it:\n1. Creates a new env with params bound to args\n2. Evaluates the body of the inner m-apply\n\nThe body of the inner m-apply contains:\n```scheme\n(if (procedure? proc)\n    (eval-sequence (procedure-body proc) (extend-environment ...))\n    (proc (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n```\n\nWhen the outer m-eval evaluates this:\n- `(procedure? proc)` → checks if proc's car is 'procedure\n- If proc is a Python function: `(pair? proc)` → False → `procedure?` returns False\n- Else branch: `(proc (car args) (car (cdr args)))` → interp.py calls it\n\nIf proc is a compound procedure (in my representation): `(pair? proc)` → True (it's a list), `(eq? (car proc) 'procedure)` → True → returns True.\n\nThen `(eval-sequence (procedure-body proc) (extend-environment ...))`:\n- `(procedure-body proc)` → `(car (cdr (cdr proc)))` → the body of the compound procedure\n\nThis all works! The key insight is that Python functions are \"opaque\" to my evaluator - I just pass them through to interp.py for application.\n\nOK wait, but what about null checks? When I do `(if (null? expr) ...)` in my m-eval, `null?` looks up in the env and gets... a Python function. `(null? expr)` → interp.py evaluates `null?` → Python lambda `lambda x: x is None` → calls it with expr. This works because `null?` is a Python function in my env.\n\nBut hold on - in my m-eval, I need to call `null?`, `pair?`, `eq?`, etc. These are Python functions stored in my environment. When m-eval's body is evaluated by interp.py, these resolve correctly.\n\nBut in the SELF-interpretATION case: the inner m-eval calls `null?`, `pair?`, etc. These are looked up by the outer m-eval in the inner's environment. They should be Python functions. And the outer m-apply just calls them (since they're not compound procedures). This works!\n\nSo the design simplifies to:\n1. 2\nenv.define(\"car\", lambda p: p.car ...)  # 1\nenv.define(\"cdr\", lambda p: p.cdr ...)  # 1\nenv.define(\"null?\", lambda x: x is None)  # 1\nenv.define(\"pair?\", lambda x: isinstance(x, Pair))  # 0\nenv.define(\"putchr\", lambda n: sys.stdout.write(chr(n)))  # 1\nenv.define(\"read\", read_sexp)  # 0\nenv.define(\"symbol\", symbol_from_list)  # 1\nenv.define(\"symbol?\", lambda x: isinstance(x, str))  # If it's a local one, it goes in the local bindings.\n\nFor my metacircular evaluator, `define` should bind in the first (innermost) frame of the environment chain. For top-level defines, the global environment is just a single frame, so it works there too.\n\nWait, but there's a subtlety with top-level defines vs internal defines. In standard Scheme, `define` at top level adds a binding to the top-level environment. But `define` inside a function body creates a local binding. In my implementation, since `define` always operates on the first frame, internal defines will correctly create a new binding in the innermost frame.\n\nFor `set!`, it should update an existing binding by searching through the environment chain.\n\nLet me also think about `lambda`. When a lambda is created, it captures the current environment. (car expr) 'quote) (car (cdr expr)))\n    ((eq? (car expr) 'if) (eval-if expr env))\n    ((eq? (car expr) 'cond) (eval-cond expr env))\n    ((eq? (car expr) 'define) (eval-define expr env))\n    ((eq? (car expr) 'set!) (eval-set! expr env))\n    ((eq? (car expr) 'lambda) (eval-lambda expr env))\n    ((eq? (car expr) 'let) (eval-let expr env))\n    ((eq? (car expr) 'begin) (eval-begin expr env))\n    ((eq? (car expr) 'progn) (eval-progn expr env))\n    ;; Application\n    (else (apply-proc (eval-expr (car expr) env)\n                      (eval-list (cdr expr) env)\n                      env))))\n```\n\nWait, there's a problem. Let me handle this differently for each built-in.\n\nActually, for `+` and `*` which take variable args, I can make them take 2 args in my evaluator and have the user program use folding. Let me simplify.\n\nActually, here's the key realization: For the metacircular evaluator, I can just NOT compile/evaluate the body of user-defined procedures in my evaluator. Instead, I can use interp.py's application mechanism directly.\n\nNo wait, I need my metacircular evaluator to be self-contained. The whole point is that eval.scm can evaluate any Scheme program, including itself.\n\nOK let me try a completely different approach. What if I DON'T put Python callables in my metacircular environment? Instead, I implement all primitives directly in Scheme.\n\nFor example:\n```scheme\n(define (my-+ . args) ...)  ; Can't do . args\n(define (my-+ a b) (+ a b))\n```\n\nAnd then in my metacircular evaluator, `+` is bound to a procedure that I create with `make-procedure` using the custom approach:\n\nWait, I can't define variable-arity functions in this Scheme. But I can define `my-+` that takes exactly 2 args:\n\n```scheme\n(define (my-+ a b) (+ a b))\n```\n\nThis creates a pair `(procedure (a b) ((+ a b)) env)` in my evaluator. When my evaluator applies it, it creates a new environment and evaluates `(+ a b)`. But wait, `+` in the new environment is the Python `+` function, not my wrapper. So when my evaluator evaluates `(+ a b)`, it looks up `+` in the environment, gets the Python function, and then needs to APPLY IT... which brings us back to the same problem!\n\nUGH. The circularity is:\n1. My evaluator evaluates `(+ a b)` where `+` is the Python function\n2. My evaluator evaluates `+` → Python function\n3. My evaluator evaluates `a`, `b` → values\n4. My evaluator needs to apply the Python function to the values\n5. But it can't call the Python function from Scheme!\n\nThe solution MUST be: In my metacircular evaluator, when I need to apply a built-in (Python function), I need to jump back to interp.py's evaluation. One way to do this is to return a special value that tells the caller to evaluate it.\n\nWait, but I can just use the `apply` trick where inside my eval.scm, I define an `apply-builtin` function that takes a function and args, and handles calling it.\n\nHERE'S THE KEY INSIGHT: I CAN call Python functions from within eval.scm because eval.scm runs in interp.py! When I write `(call-builtin proc arg1 arg2)` in eval.scm, interp.py evaluates `proc` (getting the Python function), evaluates `arg1` and `arg2`, and then calls `proc(arg1, arg2)` because proc is callable.\n\nSo I just need to define call-builtin for each arity:\n\n```scheme\n(define (call-builtin0 f) (f))\n(define (call-builtin1 f a) (f a))\n(define (call-builtin2 f a b) (f a b))\n(define (call-builtin3 f a b c) (f a b c))\n```\n\nWait, but `call-builtin2` would be a Procedure (Scheme-level), and when my evaluator evaluates `(call-builtin2 + 3 4)`, it would:\n1. Evaluate `call-builtin2` → my Scheme procedure\n2. Evaluate `+` → Python function\n3. Evaluate `3` → 3, `4` → 4\n4. Apply my Scheme procedure: bind `f` to Python `+` function, `a` to 3, `b` to 4\n5. Evaluate body `(f a b)` in the new environment\n6. Interp.py evaluates `(f a b)`: looks up `f` → Python `+`, looks up `a` → 3, `b` → 4\n7. Calls `+(3, 4)` → returns 7!\n\nYES! This works! When interp.py evaluates `(f a b)`, it does `proc = eval_expr('f', env)` getting the `+` function, then `callable(proc)` is True, so it does `proc(3, 4)`. \n\nSo I just need these trampoline functions in my eval.scm:\n\n```scheme\n(define (call0 f) (f))\n(define (call1 f a) (f a))\n(define (call2 f a b) (f a b))\n(define (call3 f a b c) (f a b c))\n(define (call4 f a b c d) (f a b c d))\n(define (call5 f a b c d e) (f a b c d e))\n```\n\nAnd then in my apply-proc, for builtins:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-builtin proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((null? args) (call0 proc))\n    ((null? (cdr args)) (call1 proc (car args)))\n    ((null? (cdr (cdr args))) (call2 proc (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ((null? (cdr (cdr (cdr args)))) (call3 proc (car args) (car (cdr args)) (car (cdr (cdr args)))))\n    ((null? (cdr (cdr (cdr (cdr args))))) (call4 proc (car args) (car (cdr args)) (car (cdr (cdr args))) (car (cdr (cdr (cdr args))))))\n    (else (error \"too many args\"))))\n```\n\nThis should work because `proc` is bound to a Python callable in the environment, and when interp.py evaluates e.g. `(call2 proc a b)`, it handles the application of proc.\n\nYES! This is the solution!\n\nNow let me think about the full evaluator structure.\n\nFor `+` and `*` which take variable args, calling them with exactly the right number of args via call-builtin should work because they're defined as `lambda *args: ...`.\n\nOK wait, but for `-`:\n```python\nenv.define(\"-\", lambda a, b=None: -a if b is None else a - b)\n```\n\nThis takes 1 or 2 args. If my `apply-builtin` calls `(call2 - a b)`, it would pass 2 args and work correctly. For 1 arg, it would call `(call1 - a)`.\n\nFor `/`:\n```python\nenv.define(\"/\", lambda a, b: a // b if ...)\n```\nThis always takes 2 args. Main loop (read file, evaluate expressions)\n\nLet me also think about `define` syntax:\n\nSimple define: `(define name value)`\nFunction define shorthand: `(define (f x y) body1 body2 ...)`\n\nFor the function define shorthand, I need to destructure it:\n```scheme\n(define (f x y) body)\n```\nbecomes:\n```scheme\n(define f (lambda (x y) body))\n```\n\nIn terms of pair structure:\n```\n(define (f x y) body)\n```\nThe car is 'define\nThe cdr is ((f x y) body)\nThe cdr.car is (f x y) - a pair\nThe cdr.cdr is (body)\n```\n\nSo to detect the shorthand:\n```scheme\n((eq? (car (cdr expr)))\n      ;; Function definition shorthand\n      (let ((name (car (car (cdr expr))))\n            (params (cdr (car (cdr expr))))\n            (body (cdr (cdr expr))))\n        (define-in-env name (make-lambda params body env) env))\n      ;; Simple definition\n      (define-in-env (car (cdr expr))\n                     (eval-expr (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env)\n                     env)))\n```\n\nWait but `(cdr (car (cdr expr)))` = params? Let me be more careful.\n\n`expr` = `(Pair 'define (Pair (Pair 'f (Pair 'x (Pair 'y '()))) (Pair body-expr '())))`\n\nSo:\n- `(cdr expr)` = `(Pair (Pair 'f ...) (Pair body-expr '()))` = `((f x y) body-expr)`\n- `(car (cdr expr))` = `(Pair 'f (Pair 'x (Pair 'y '())))` = `(f x y)`\n- `(car (car (cdr expr)))` = `'f` = the function name\n- `(cdr (car (cdr expr)))` = `(Pair 'x (Pair 'y '()))` = `(x y)` = the parameter list\n- `(cdr (cdr expr))` = `(Pair body-expr '())` = `(body-expr)` = the body (list of expressions)\n\nSo:\n```\nname = (car (car (cdr expr)))\nparams = (cdr (car (cdr expr)))\nbody = (cdr (cdr expr))\n```\n\nFor the lambda creation: \n```\n(make-lambda params body env)\n```\nwhere `params` is the list of parameter symbols and `body` is the list of body expressions.\n\nFor `lambda`:\n```scheme\n(define (eval-lambda expr env)\n  (make-procedure (car (cdr expr)) (cdr (cdr expr)) env))\n```\n\nWait, `expr` = `(Pair 'lambda (Pair params (Pair body1 (Pair body2 '()))))` where params is `(Pair 'x (Pair 'y '()))`.\n\nSo:\n- `(car (cdr expr))` = params = `(x y)`\n- `(cdr (cdr expr))` = body list = `(body1 body2 ...)`\n\nHmm, but `lambda` body is a sequence, like in `(lambda (x) body1 body2)`. Let me design it carefully.\n\n### `eval-begin` (and `progn`)\n\n```scheme\n(define (eval-begin expr env)\n  (let ((body (cdr expr)))\n    (if (null? body)\n        '()\n        (if (null? And in interp.py, `'()` is None. `(eq? None #f)` would be `None == False` which in Python is `False`. So `(not (eq? None #f))` = `(not False)` = `True`. \n\n### `eval-cond`\n\n```scheme\n(define (eval-cond expr env)\n  (let ((clauses (cdr expr)))\n    (eval-cond-clauses clauses env)))\n\n(define (eval-cond-clauses clauses env)\n  (if (null? clauses)\n      '()\n      (let ((clause (car clauses)))\n        (let ((test (car clause))\n              (body (cdr clause)))\n          (if (or (eq? test 'else) (not (eq? vars)\n      '()\n      (cons (cons (car vars) (car vals))\n            (pair-up (cdr vars) (cdr vals)))))\n\n(define (lookup var env)\n  (if (null? env)\n      (error \"undefined variable\")\n      (let ((frame (car env)))\n        (let ((binding (assoc var frame)))\n          (if binding\n              (cdr binding)\n              (lookup var (cdr env)))))))\n\n(define (define-in-env var val env)\n  (if (null? And `fread` reads from the file handle. When `fread` reaches EOF, it returns `None` which is `'()` in Scheme. So `(null? expr)` checks for EOF.\n\nLet me also handle the case where fopen fails:\n```scheme\n(if file-handle\n    (eval-file file-handle global-env)\n    (error \"cannot open file\"))\n```\n\nBut `#f` is falsy and `\"file_1\"` is truthy, so this should work.\n\nActually wait - `if` in interp.py checks `condition is not False`. In Scheme, `if` treats everything except `#f` as truthy. But in interp.py, `if` checks `condition is not False`. So `0` is truthy, `'()` (None) is truthy, empty string would be... well, strings are `String` objects, so `\"\"` → `String(\"\")` and `String(\"\") is not False` → True. So it's truthy. Good.\n\n### More helper functions\n\nI need to define `map`, `cadr`, `cddr`, etc.\n\nWait, actually, for the evaluator implementation, I don't need `map` in the sense of the higher-order function. I need helper functions to manipulate pairs and lists. Let me use explicit recursion instead of map.\n\nLet me write the complete eval.scm now.\n\nWait, I need to be careful about one more thing: the `read` function in the metacircular evaluator. When the interpreted program calls `(read)`, it should read from stdin. Good.\n\nOK, one more thing: when the program in the file uses `read`, it should read from the remaining stdin. Since the built-in `read` is available in the metacircular environment, this works naturally.\n\nNow let me also think about `define` in the context of the metacircular evaluator. When the interpreted file does `(define x 5)`, my evaluator's `eval-define` calls `define-in-env` which adds the binding to the first (innermost) frame. For the global environment, the first frame IS the global frame (since we start with `(list '())`), so defines go there.\n\nFor internal defines (inside lambda bodies), my `apply-user` extends the environment, so `define-in-env` adds to the innermost frame (the new frame for that function). This gives us lexical scoping!\n\nNow, there's a subtle issue with internal defines. In standard Scheme, internal defines are hoisted to the top of the body. But in my implementation, they're executed in order. This might cause issues with mutual recursion among internal defines.\n\nLooking at the test file `nested_defines.scm`:\n```scheme\n(define (test-mutual n)\n  (define (even? x) (if (= x 0) #t (odd?
